Full answer
-----------

Answer: $46,026

How the total is reached

| What triggers the notice | How many | Cost each | Subtotal | |---|---|---|---| | SNUN for reclaiming the 90 %‑control oxide | 1 | $45,496 (large‑business SNUN: $37,000 user fee + ~$8,496 prep/registration) | $45,496 | | Export notices (40 CFR 721.20 / TSCA §12(b)) | 5 | ~$106 (one‑time notice per chemical per country) | $530 | | Total | | | $46,026 |

Why only one SNUN is needed The 2026 proposed SNURs (40 CFR §§ 721.12219 and 721.12220) make reclaiming/recycling these cobalt‑lithium‑manganese‑nickel oxides a significant new use unless the recycler uses dust controls with ≥ 99 % combined capture/control efficiency (§ (a)(2)(iii)).

- The oxide reclaimed with 90 % efficiency does not meet the exception → a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN) is required.
- The oxide reclaimed with 99 % efficiency meets the exception → no SNUN.

Because the recycler is “large,” it pays the large‑business fee ($37,000) plus preparation costs, totaling $45,496 per SNUN (Doc 7, Unit I.D.1).

Why five export notices Export of any chemical subject to these SNURs requires a one‑time TSCA §12(b) notification for the first export to each country (Doc 7, Unit I.C.2; I.D.2). The recycler ships:

- Both oxides → Japan and Chile (2 substances × 2 countries = 4 notices)
- One oxide → Thailand (1 substance × 1 country = 1 notice)

The Thai buyer’s partial Chinese‑city ownership (10 % equity + 20 % convertible notes = potential 30 % control) is a FEOC red herring; the prompt says to bar FEOCs, so the shipment still occurs and still needs the notice. Two Japanese buyers still equal only one country (Japan).

5 notices × ~$106 = $530.

Grand total $45,496 + $530 = $46,026.

Full answer
-----------

Answer: $45,920

How the count is reached

| Activity | Is it a “significant new use” under the proposed SNURs (§§ 721.12219 & 721.12220)? | Notification required? | Cost | |---|---|---|---| | Reclaim oxide A with 90 % dust control | Yes – the SNUR requires ≥ 99 % capture/control for any processing (including recycling/reclaiming). | 1 SNUN (large business) | $45,496 | | Reclaim oxide B with 99 % dust control | No – meets the 99 % control exemption. | None | $0 | | Export oxide A → Japan (2 buyers, 1 country) | Export of a SNUR‑covered substance requires a one‑time TSCA § 12(b) notice per country. | 1 export notice | $106 | | Export oxide A → Chile | Same as above. | 1 export notice | $106 | | Export oxide B → Japan | Same as above. | 1 export notice | $106 | | Export oxide B → Chile | Same as above. | 1 export notice | $106 | | Export (either) → Thai buyer | Barred – the buyer is 10 % owned by a Chinese city (a subnational government of a covered nation) plus convertible notes for another 20 %. Under DOE’s final FEOC interpretation (Doc. 4), contingent equity is counted, so the government holds 30 % (> 25 %) → the buyer is a FEOC. “Barring FEOCs” removes this shipment. | None | $0 |

Cost math

- 1 large‑business SNUN submission = $45,496 (includes the $37,000 user fee + preparation/CDX costs; see Doc. 7, Unit I.D.1)
- 4 allowed export notifications × ~$106 each = $424 (Doc. 7, Unit I.D.2)

Total = $45,496 + $424 = $45,920

Named Doc 7, Doc 4

Full answer
-----------

The two proposed SNURs are for the two generic cobalt-lithium-manganese-nickel oxides (40 CFR §§ 721.12219 and 721.12220, PMNs P-25-73/P-25-152 and P-25-137/P-25-151).

1. Significant New Use Notices (SNUNs) required

- Reclaiming/recycling is “processing” and must use dust controls with ≥99 % combined capture/control efficiency.
- The recycler uses 99 % for one oxide → conforms to the order/SNUR, so no SNUN.
- The recycler uses 90 % for the other oxide → this is a significant new use, so one SNUN is required.
- The recycler is a large business, so the SNUN costs $45,496 (includes the $37,000 user fee, CDX registration, and preparation).

2. Export notifications required (40 CFR 721.20 / TSCA §12(b)) A one-time notice is required for each chemical substance exported to each country (cost ≈ $106 per notice).

Exports:

- Both oxides → Japan (2 notices)
- Both oxides → Chile (2 notices)
- One oxide → Thailand (1 notice)

That’s 5 export notices × $106 = $530.

(The Thai buyer’s 10 % direct + 20 % convertible Chinese-city ownership reaches the 25 % “controlled by” FEOC threshold under the 2024 DOE interpretation, but the prompt says “Barring FEOCs,” so that export is still counted for notification purposes and no extra FEOC-related filing cost applies.)

3. Total cost

- 1 SNUN: $45,496
- 5 export notices: $530

Altogether: $45,496 + $530 = $46,026

Full answer
-----------

Answer: $46,026

How the number is reached

1. SNUR triggers (Doc. 7, 40 CFR §§ 721.12219/721.12220)

- Reclaiming/recycling is a “significant new use” unless dust controls have ≥ 99 % combined capture/control efficiency (§ (a)(2)(iii)).
- 90 %‑efficient line → fails the exception ⇒ one SNUN is required for that oxide.
- 99 %‑efficient line → meets the exception ⇒ no SNUN for that oxide.
- The recycler is “large,” so the full SNUN cost applies: $45,496 (includes $37,000 user fee + preparation/CDX costs) (Doc. 7, Unit I.D.1).

1. Export notifications (TSCA § 12(b); 40 CFR part 707, subpart D)

- Any export of a substance covered by the proposed SNUR requires a one‑time notice per chemical per country (Doc. 7, Unit I.C.2; cost ≈ $106 each) (Doc. 7, Unit I.D.2).
- Countries involved: Japan, Chile, and Thailand.
- Both oxides go to Japan and Chile ⇒ 2 chemicals × 2 countries = 4 notices.
- Only one oxide goes to Thailand ⇒ 1 notice.
- Total export notices: 5 × $106 = $530.

1. FEOC distractor (Doc. 4)

- The Thai buyer is 10 % owned by a Chinese city government plus notes convertible to another 20 % ⇒ 30 % cumulative interest, exceeding the 25 % “owned/controlled” threshold, so it is a FEOC.
- The problem says “Barring FEOCs” ⇒ set that issue aside; the Thailand export still counts for notification purposes.

Total cost

- 1 SNUN: $45,496
- 5 export notices: $530

Altogether: $45,496 + $530 = $46,026

Full answer
-----------

Total EPA Notification Cost: $46,026

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Significant New Use Notification (SNUN) — $45,496

Under the 2026 SNURs (40 CFR 721.12219 and 721.12220), processing (including recycling/reclaiming) is a significant new use unless done with dust controls achieving a 99% minimum combined capture and control efficiency.

- Substance processed at 90% dust controls → falls below the 99% threshold → significant new use → SNUN required
- Substance processed at 99% dust controls → meets the threshold → not a significant new use → no SNUN

Since the recycler is a large business, the cost is $45,496 per SNUN (including the $37,000 user fee). Only one SNUN is needed.

2. Export Notifications (TSCA § 12(b)) — $530

A one-time notice is required per substance per country at approximately $106 each:

| Destination | Substance 1 | Substance 2 | Notifications | |---|---|---|---| | Japan (2 buyers) | ✅ | ✅ | 2 | | Chile (1 buyer) | ✅ | ✅ | 2 | | Thailand (1 buyer) | ✅ (one substance only) | — | 1 | | Total | | | 5 |

5 × $106 = $530

3. FEOC Analysis of the Thai Buyer

Under DOE's FEOC interpretive rule (Document 4), equity interests include contingent equity interests (convertible instruments), with the reasonably determinable amount included in the 25% calculation, "without consideration of whether conversion is imminent":

$$10\% \text{ (equity)} + 20\% \text{ (convertible notes)} = 30\% \geq 25\%$$

The Thai buyer is a FEOC — controlled by the PRC government (a covered nation). However, this does not alter TSCA export notification requirements; the per-country export notification for Thailand is still required.

Full answer

Calculating Total Notification Costs

1. SNUN (Significant New Use Notification) — $45,496

The SNURs require dust controls with ≥99% combined capture and control efficiency for any processing (explicitly including recycling/reclamation). The recycler's operation with 90%-efficient dust controls falls below this threshold, constituting a significant new use and triggering one SNUN. The oxide processed with 99%-efficient controls meets the requirement and needs no SNUN.

As a large business, the estimated cost per SNUN is $45,496 (including the $37,000 user fee).

2. Export Notifications (TSCA §12(b)) — $530

Any export of a substance subject to a SNUR requires a one-time notification per substance per destination country at ~$106 each:

| Substance | Countries exported to | Notifications | |---|---|---| | Oxide A (e.g., §721.12219) | Japan, Chile, Thailand | 3 | | Oxide B (e.g., §721.12220) | Japan, Chile | 2 |

(Two Japanese buyers = still one notification per country.)

5 notifications × $106 = $530

3. FEOC Note (no additional notification cost)

The Thai buyer is indeed a FEOC: the Chinese city (subnational government of a covered nation) holds 10% equity plus contingent convertible notes worth another 20%, totaling 30% equity interest — exceeding the 25% independent-metric threshold. However, FEOC status affects DOE grant eligibility and the 30D tax credit; it does not create a separate TSCA notification obligation.

Full answer
-----------

Calculating Total EPA Notification Costs

1. SNUN (Significant New Use Notification)

Both substances carry the restriction that processing (which expressly includes recycling/reclaiming) requires dust controls with ≥99% capture and control efficiency.

| Substance | Dust Controls | Compliant? | SNUN Needed? | |-----------|--------------|------------|--------------| | One oxide | 90% | No (< 99%) | Yes | | Other oxide | 99% | Yes (= 99%) | No |

Only 1 SNUN is required (for the 90%-efficient one). Per the economic analysis in the proposed rule, the cost for a large business submitter is $45,496 (including the $37,000 user fee, CDX registration, etc.).

2. Export Notifications (TSCA § 12(b))

Any person exporting a substance subject to a SNUR must file a one-time notification per substance per country. The two substances are distinct (721.12219 and 721.12220), and the export destinations per substance are:

| Substance | Japan | Chile | Thailand | Notifications | |-----------|-------|-------|----------|---------------| | Substance 1 (both to Japan/Chile, one to Thailand) | 1 | 1 | 1 | 3 | | Substance 2 (both to Japan/Chile) | 1 | 1 | — | 2 |

Two Japanese buyers count as one notification (one per country per substance). Total = 5 export notifications × $106 = $530.

3. FEOC Analysis (Thai Buyer)

The Thai buyer — 10% owned by a Chinese city (subnational government of a covered nation) with notes convertible into another 20% — totals 30% equity interest (including contingent equity, per DOE's FEOC guidance), exceeding the 25% threshold. This makes the Thai buyer a FEOC. However, TSCA § 12(b) export notification is required regardless of FEOC status (FEOC rules govern DOE grants and IRA tax credits, not TSCA notifications).

Total Notification Cost

| Notification Type | Count | Unit Cost | Total | |-------------------|-------|-----------|-------| | SNUN | 1 | $45,496 | $45,496 | | Export notification | 5 | $106 | $530 |

$$\boxed{\$46{,}026}$$

**Exports**: Persons who export or intend to export a chemical substance that is the subject of a SNUR are subject to TSCA section 12(b) export notification requirements. Each export to a particular country requires a one-time notification.

Let me think about FEOC considerations. The Thai buyer is 10% owned by a Chinese city holding notes convertible into another 20%.

From Document 4 (DOE final interpretive rule on FEOC):
- "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction" means 25% or more of board seats, voting rights, or equity interest, each evaluated independently.
- "Equity interests" includes contingent equity interests (consistent with CFIUS regulations31 CFR 800.207).
- The amount of contingent interest that can be reasonably determined should be included in the 25% control calculation.

So the Thai buyer: 10% owned by Chinese city + notes convertible into another 20%. The 20% convertible notes would be contingent equity interests. DOE says contingent equity interests should be included. So 10% + 20% = 30%, which exceeds 25%.

Full answer
-----------

Only the Japanese buyer is actually non-FEOC: the Polish firm is >25% owned by a Chinese government entity (50%), and the Indonesian subsidiary is controlled by a China-incorporated parent (thus an FEOC subsidiary). For the Japanese firm, the province holds only 20% of board votes and lacks a board majority, so it falls below the 25% FEOC thresholds.

With 90% dust capture, the recycler misses the typical ≥95% (or full-exemption) control threshold in the SNUR, so reclaiming the oxide is a significant new use requiring one TSCA §5 Significant New Use Notice—regardless of the single eligible export destination. For a large company, the 2026 TSCA notice fee is $37,000 (one SNUN covers the activity), so notifying EPA costs $37,000 altogether.

Short answer: Only the Indonesian subsidiary is a permissible non‑FEOC buyer. Because the recycler’s 90 % dust capture fails the 99 % SNUR requirement, it must file a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN). As a large firm that costs $45,496 (including the $37,000 TSCA user fee). Exporting to Indonesia also requires a one‑time TSCA §12(b) export notice ($106).

Total cost to notify EPA: $45,602 ($45,496 + $106).

The rule requires ≥99 % combined capture/control efficiency for any manufacturing/processing (including recycling/reclaiming). 90 % is a “significant new use” that requires a 90‑day SNUN before commencing. | | Cost of the SNUN | Document 7, Unit I.D.2: large‑business SNUN = $45,496 (preparation + CDX registration + $37,000 user fee under 40 CFR 700.45). Final answer: $45,496 (one large‑business SNUN) + $212 (two export notices) = $45,708. | Your answer (gold): $45,708

Full answer
-----------

Answer: about $45,708 in total

Why a notice is required at all The 2026 proposed SNURs for the cobalt‑lithium‑manganese‑nickel oxides (40 CFR §§ 721.12219/721.12220, Doc 7) make “processing (including recycling/reclaiming) … except with dust controls having an overall minimum combined capture and control efficiency of 99 %” a significant new use. The recycler’s 90 % efficient controls fall short, so reclaiming the oxide is a significant new use and requires a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN) (Doc 7, § III.B).

Cost of the SNUN (large business) Doc 7 (§ I.D.1) states that for large submitters the all‑in cost of a SNUN—including preparation, CDX registration, and the user fee—is $45,496 (the user fee alone is $37,000 under 40 CFR 700.45(c)(2)(ii)).

Which prospects are “non‑FEOC”? (Doc 4, § B) To be a FEOC an entity must be a “foreign entity” and either (i) subject to the jurisdiction of a covered‑nation government, or (ii) ≥ 25 % owned/controlled/directed by that government.

| Prospect | FEOC analysis | Result | |---|---|---| | Chinese city’s 50 %‑owned Polish firm | The Chinese city is a subnational government of the PRC (covered nation). It holds 50 % equity—well above the 25 % threshold—so the Polish firm is controlled by a covered‑nation government. | FEOC | | Private Shanghai company’s Indonesian subsidiary | The Shanghai parent is private (not government). The subsidiary is organized/operating in Indonesia, not in China, and is not shown to be ≥ 25 % government‑held. | Not FEOC | | Japanese firm (Chinese province chooses 2 of 5 directors, casting 20 % of board votes) | The province is a covered‑nation government, but its board‑vote share is 20 %, below the 25 % control threshold; no ≥ 25 % equity/voting rights are shown. The firm is in Japan. | Not FEOC |

So the recycler will export only to the Indonesian subsidiary and the Japanese firm—two countries.

Cost of export notifications Under TSCA § 12(b) (40 CFR part 707, subpart D), a one‑time export notice is required for the first export to each country of a chemical subject to a SNUR. Doc 7 (§ I.D.2) puts that cost at about $106 per country.

- Indonesia: $106
- Japan: $106
- Export notices subtotal: $212

Total notification cost $45,496 (one large‑business SNUN) + $212 (two export notices) = $45,708.

Let's determine FEOC status for each.

Recall interpretation in doc4 (final rule effective May 6, 2024):
- Foreign entity means government of foreign country; natural person not US citizen/permanent resident; partnership/corporation organized under laws of or principal place of business in foreign country; or US entity owned/controlled/direction of foreign entity.
- Government of foreign country includes national/subnational government, agency/instrumentality, dominant/ruling political party (e.g., CCP), or current/former senior foreign political figure.
- Subject to jurisdiction: incorporated/domiciled/principal place of business in covered nation (China, Russia, NK, Iran); or with respect to critical minerals/components/materials, entity engages in extraction/processing/recycling/manufacturing/assembly in covered nation.
- Owned by, controlled by, or subject to direction: 25% or more of board seats, voting rights, or equity interest cumulatively held by other entity (including government of covered nation), directly or indirectly; OR effective control via licensing/contracting.

Also indirect control rules:
- If parent holds 50%+ of subsidiary, they are treated equivalent.
- If parent holds less than 50%, indirect ownership attributed proportionately.

Need apply to each prospect.

A. Chinese city's 50%-owned Polish firm.
The firm is Polish (organized in Poland? principal place of business in Poland?). But 50% owned by a Chinese city government. The Chinese city is a subnational government of PRC (covered nation). So the government of a covered nation holds 50% equity interest. The threshold for control is 25% or more. So 50% >25%, therefore the Polish firm is "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the government of a foreign country that is a covered nation. Also is it a foreign entity? Yes, organized under laws of Poland (foreign country). So it meets both: foreign entity and controlled by covered nation government. Therefore it IS a FEOC.

But wait, there's also the "subject to jurisdiction" prong: The firm is incorporated in Poland, not in covered nation, and if its activities (recycling?) not in covered nation? But it doesn't matter because it meets the control prong.

Thus prospect A is FEOC, not non-FEOC.

So paragraph (iv) only applies to US-organized entities being considered foreign entities if controlled by foreign entities. It doesn't apply to foreign-organized entities.

Answer: $45,708

How the answer is reached

1. The recycler’s activity is a “significant new use”

The 2026 proposed SNURs (40 CFR §§ 721.12219 & 721.12220) cover the cobalt‑lithium‑manganese‑nickel oxide family. They allow reclaiming/recycling only when dust controls have “an overall minimum combined capture and control efficiency of 99 %.”

- The recycler uses 90 % efficient controls → does not meet the exception.
- Reclaiming is therefore a significant new use, so a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN) must be filed with EPA before the activity starts.

1. Cost of the SNUN (large business)

The economic analysis in the proposed rule states:

- Large‑business SNUN submission cost = $45,496 (includes preparation, CDX registration, and the $37,000 user fee).

1. Which buyers are non‑FEOCs? (Apply the Dec. 2024 final FEOC interpretation, 89 FR 37079)

An entity is a FEOC if it is “owned by, controlled by, or subject to the jurisdiction or direction of” a covered‑nation government, with control defined as ≥25 % of board seats, voting rights, or equity interest (cumulatively).

| Prospect | Analysis | FEOC? | |---|---|---| | Chinese city’s 50 %‑owned Polish firm | A Chinese city (sub‑national government) holds 50 % equity → well above the 25 % control threshold. | Yes (FEOC) | | Private Shanghai company’s Indonesian subsidiary | Parent is private, not government‑owned; subsidiary is in Indonesia (not incorporated in or operating in China for this purpose) → no government control ≥25 % and not subject to Chinese jurisdiction for this activity. | No (non‑FEOC) | | Japanese firm; Chinese province picks 2 of 5 directors, casting 20 % of board votes | The province’s voting rights are 20 % (<25 %). Even though it holds 2 seats, the problem frames the government’s power as 20 % of board votes, which falls below the 25 % bright‑line. | No (non‑FEOC) |

So the recycler exports to two non‑FEOC destinations: Indonesia and Japan.

1. Export‑notification costs

Under TSCA § 12(b) / 40 CFR part 707, a one‑time notice is required for the first export to each country of a substance subject to a SNUR.

- Cost per country notice ≈ $106.
- Two countries → 2 × $106 = $212.

1. Total notification cost

- SNUN (large recycler): $45,496
- Export notices (Indonesia + Japan): $212

$45,496 + $212 = $45,708

Answer: $45,708

How the answer is reached

1. The 2026 SNUR applies – Document 7 proposes SNURs for two “cobalt lithium manganese nickel oxide, metals‑doped” substances (40 CFR 721.12219 and 721.12220). For processing/recycling, the Order/SNUR requires ≥99 % combined dust capture and control efficiency. The recycler uses 90 %, so reclaiming the oxide is a “significant new use” that requires a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN) (Document 7, Unit III.B/C).
2. Cost of the SNUN – Document 7 (Unit I.D.1) estimates a large‑business SNUN costs $45,496 (includes preparation, CDX registration, and the $37,000 user fee).
3. FEOC check for the three prospects (Document 4, final interpretive rule; 25 % control threshold for board seats, voting rights, or equity):

- Chinese city’s 50 %‑owned Polish firm – The Chinese city is a covered‑nation (PRC) subnational government. It holds 50 % equity (>25 %) → FEOC. Not exported to.
- Private Shanghai company’s Indonesian subsidiary – The parent is private (no government control). The subsidiary is in Indonesia and is not automatically a FEOC just because its parent is in China (Document 4, Section III.D) → Non‑FEOC. Exported to Indonesia.
- Japanese firm where a Chinese province appoints 2 of 5 directors but casts only 20 % of board votes – The province holds only 20 % of voting/board influence, below the 25 % control threshold → Non‑FEOC. Exported to Japan.

So there are two non‑FEOC destinations: Indonesia and Japan.

1. Export notification costs – Under 40 CFR 721.20 (Document 7, Unit I.C.2), exporting a SNUR‑covered substance requires a one‑time TSCA §12(b) notice per country. The per‑notice cost is about $106 (Document 7, Unit I.D.2).

2 countries × $106 = $212.

1. Total notification cost

- 1 SNUN: $45,496
- 2 export notices: $212
- Total = $45,708

Full answer

Answer: $45,602

Why only one buyer is “non‑FEOC”

Under DOE’s final FEOC interpretation (Document 4, 40 CFR § 721 context):

| Prospect | FEOC analysis | |---|---| | Chinese city’s 50%‑owned Polish firm | The city (a subnational government of China, a covered nation) holds 50% of the equity/voting interest. That is ≥25%, so the firm is FEOC (control prong). | | Private Shanghai company’s Indonesian subsidiary | The parent is incorporated in China, so it is a FEOC by jurisdiction, but the subsidiary is in Indonesia and is not automatically a FEOC just because its parent is (Document 4, § V.E, “Subject to the Jurisdiction” discussion). The parent is private (not the government), so the subsidiary is not “owned/controlled by a covered‑nation government.” → Non‑FEOC. | | Japanese firm – Chinese province picks 2 of 5 directors (20% of board votes) | The province is part of China’s government. The rule evaluates board seats, voting rights, and equity independently (Document 4, § V.E(i)). Two of five directors = 40% of board seats, which exceeds the 25% threshold, even though the voting‑rights share is 20%. So the firm is FEOC via the board‑seat metric. |

So the recycler can export only to Indonesia (the Indonesian subsidiary).

What triggers EPA notification

The proposed SNUR (Document 7, 40 CFR § 721.12219/12220) makes it a significant new use to reclaim/process the covered oxide without dust controls achieving ≥99% combined capture/control efficiency. The recycler’s 90% controls fall below that cutoff, so it must submit a Significant New Use Notice (SNUN) before reclaiming.

Because the chemical is subject to the SNUR, TSCA § 12(b) also requires a one‑time export notification for the first shipment to each country (Document 7, § I.D.2).

Cost calculation

- SNUN (large business): $45,496

(includes $37,000 user fee + preparation/CDX costs; Document 7, Executive Summary, § I.D.1)

- Export notifications: 1 country (Indonesia) × ~$106 = $106

(Document 7, Executive Summary, § I.D.2)

Total: $45,496 + $106 = $45,602

From the SNURs (Document 7), the significant new uses include:
- Manufacturing, processing (including recycling) or use of the substance **except with the use of dust controls with an overall minimum combined capture and control efficiency of 99%**
- Other restrictions (no use other than battery manufacture, labeling requirements, disposal restrictions, release to air/water limits, etc.)

The recycler uses **90%-efficient dust controls**. The SNUR requires **99%** dust controls. This means the recycler's dust controls don't meet the requirement. So recycling/reclaiming the substance with 90%-efficient dust controls constitutes a **significant new use** requiring a SNUN (Significant New Use Notification).

Also, the recycler is "exporting" - but the SNUR's significant new uses are about manufacturing, processing (which includes recycling), and use. Exporting is covered by TSCA section 12(b) export notification requirements separately.

Docs: — · Note: —

Final answer: Final answer: $45,602. | Your answer (gold): $45,708

Full answer
-----------

Step 1 — Does the recycler need a SNUN, and what does that cost?

Under the 2026 proposed SNURs (40 CFR 721.12219/721.12220), "processing" expressly includes "recycling or reclaiming substances from batteries or other items containing the PMN substance," and the protective measures require "dust controls with an overall minimum combined capture and control efficiency of 99%." The article exemption also lapses once the article is "shredded or otherwise handled such that there is potential for exposure to or release of the substance" — exactly what reclamation does.

Reclaiming with 90% dust controls therefore does not conform to the Order's protective measures, making it a significant new use requiring a SNUN at least 90 days before commencing. The recycler is a large business, so:

- SNUN cost = $45,496 (this figure already includes preparation, CDX registration, and the $37,000 user fee).

Step 2 — Which export "prospects" are actually non-FEOC? (DOE's FEOC interpretation, Doc. 2)

(a) Polish firm 50%-owned by a Chinese city — "Government of a foreign country" expressly includes subnational governments (e.g., a city). 50% ≥ the 25% board seats/voting rights/equity threshold → FEOC (controlled by a covered-nation government). ❌ Not a prospect.

(b) Indonesian subsidiary of a private Shanghai company — The Shanghai parent is itself a FEOC (subject to PRC jurisdiction), but a subsidiary is not automatically a FEOC; it must independently be subject to covered-nation jurisdiction or controlled by a covered-nation government. The parent is private, and DOE expressly states it "would not factor in any voting share, equity interest, or board seats held by an entity that is a FEOC solely by virtue of being subject to the covered nation's jurisdiction." → Non-FEOC. ✅

(c) Japanese firm; a Chinese province picks 2 of 5 directors (20% of board votes) — The test is disjunctive: 25%+ of board seats, or voting rights, or equity interest. Two of five directors = 40% of board seats, which clears 25% on its own; the 20% voting figure is a red herring. → FEOC. ❌ Not a prospect.

Result: exactly one genuine non-FEOC destination — Indonesia (one country).

Step 3 — Export notification cost

TSCA §12(b)/40 CFR part 721.20 require a one-time notice for the first export to each particular country, at ~$106 per notification.

- Export notifications = 1 × $106 = $106

Total

$45,496 (SNUN, large business) + $106 (one export notice, Indonesia) = $45,602

Final answer: $45,602.

- The proposed SNURs require persons to notify EPA at least 90 days before commencing manufacture or processing for a significant new use.
- Costs: estimated $45,496 per SNUN submission for large business submitters, $14,976 for small business submitters. Large business → $45,496.
- Export notification: estimated cost approximately $106 per unit (one-time notice to EPA for first export or intended export to a particular country).

Full answer
-----------

Analysis

FEOC Status of Each Prospect (per DOE's Final Interpretive Rule, Doc. 4)

1. Polish firm — 50% owned by a Chinese city → FEOC ✗ A Chinese city is a subnational government of a covered nation (PRC) under §V.C(i). The Polish firm is a foreign entity organized under Polish law. At 50% ownership, it exceeds the 25% cumulative control threshold in §V.E(i) (board seats, voting rights, or equity). It is "owned by, controlled by" the government of a covered nation.

2. Indonesian subsidiary of a private Shanghai company → NOT a FEOC ✓ The Shanghai parent is a FEOC under the jurisdiction prong (§V.D(i)—incorporated in/PPB in the PRC). But per §III.D, "subsidiaries of the FEOC are not automatically considered to be FEOCs solely based on their parent being a covered nation jurisdictional entity." The Indonesian subsidiary is not organized in the PRC, not produced within the PRC, and—since the Shanghai company is private—there is no indication of 25% government control flowing through. It is not a FEOC.

3. Japanese firm — Chinese province picks 2 of 5 directors, casting 20% of board votes → NOT a FEOC ✓ DOE clarified (§III.E.i) that "the value of a board seat to equal the value of its voting power on the board." The two province-appointed seats carry only 20% of board voting power. The 25% threshold is evaluated independently per metric (§V.E). Board-seat metric = 20% < 25%. With no other metric exceeding 25%, the firm is not controlled by a covered-nation government.

Notification Cost

The provided documents contain no "2026 significant-new-use proposal" for a "covered oxide," no SNUR fee schedule, and no TSCA notification cost associated with exports to any of these prospects. The FEOC framework (Docs. 2 & 4) is administered by DOE and Treasury/IRS—not EPA. Exports also generally do not trigger TSCA §5 notification.

The total cost of notifying EPA is $0.

Full answer
-----------

Cost of Notifying EPA: $45,602

Step 1 — Does the recycler need a Significant New Use Notification (SNUN)?

The 2026 SNURs (Doc. 6, §§ 721.12219 and 721.12220) require that any manufacturing, processing (including recycling/reclaiming), or use of the covered cobalt–lithium–manganese–nickel oxide be conducted with dust controls achieving at least 99% combined capture-and-control efficiency. The recycler operates at only 90%, so its activity is a significant new use (non-conformance with the protective measures of the underlying TSCA Orders). A SNUN must be filed before the activity begins.

Cost for a large business: $45,496 per SNUN (includes preparation, CDX registration, and the $37,000 user fee).

Step 2 — FEOC screening of the three prospects (Doc. 2)

| Prospect | Analysis | FEOC? | |---|---|---| | Chinese city's 50%-owned Polish firm | Foreign entity (Polish corporation). A subnational Chinese government is a "government of a foreign country" (covered nation). 50 % equity ≥ 25 % control threshold → "owned by" the covered-nation government. | Yes ❌ | | Private Shanghai company's Indonesian subsidiary | Indonesian entity = foreign entity. Not incorporated in a covered nation. Parent is private (not government-controlled). Although the parent is a FEOC by virtue of jurisdiction, subsidiaries of jurisdictional FEOCs are not automatically FEOCs, and ownership by a jurisdiction-only FEOC is excluded from the 25 % cumulative-control calculation. | No ✅ | | Japanese firm — Chinese province picks 2 of 5 directors (20 % board votes) | Foreign entity (Japanese corporation). A Chinese province is a covered-nation government. 2 of 5 board seats = 40 % of board seats ≥ 25 % threshold. The "or" in the rule means any one criterion (board seats, voting rights, or equity) suffices; 20 % board votes is irrelevant because 40 % board seats already triggers the threshold. | Yes ❌ |

Only the Indonesian subsidiary is truly non-FEOC, so the recycler exports to one country (Indonesia).

Step 3 — Export notification (TSCA § 12(b))

One-time notification per country: $106 × 1 = $106.

Total

| Item | Cost | |---|---| | SNUN (large business) | $45,496 | | Export notification (Indonesia) | $106 | | Total | $45,602 |

The question asks: Once EPA's 2026 significant-new-use proposal is final, a large recycler reclaims a covered oxide with 90%-efficient dust controls, exporting to non-FEOC prospects among three entities. What would notifying EPA cost it altogether?

Let me break this down:

1. **EPA's 2026 SNUR (Document 6)**: This is the proposed rule for "Significant New Use Rules on Certain Chemical Substances (26-2)" covering cobalt lithium manganese nickel oxide, metals-doped. The SNURs require:
   - Dust controls with an overall minimum combined capture and control efficiency of **99%** (not 90%)
   - The recycler is using 90%-efficient dust controls, which is BELOW the 99% requirement
   - This means the recycler is NOT complying with the protective measures2. **Processing includes recycling**: The SNUR explicitly states "all processing includes recycling or reclaiming substances from batteries or other items containing the PMN substance" is subject to the dust controls requirement.

3. Since the recycler uses 90% dust controls instead of 99%, this is a **significant new use** because it does not conform to the restrictions imposed by the underlying TSCA Orders. Therefore, the recycler would need to submit a **SNUN (Significant New Use Notification)**.

4. **The recycler is "large"** (not a small business), so the cost would be:
   - Estimated cost per SNUN submission for large business: **$45,496**

Hmm, wait. Let me re-read. "A private Shanghai company's Indonesian subsidiary" - the word "subsidiary" implies control/ownership, but without a specific percentage, we can't be certain it meets the 25% threshold. However, the word "subsidiary" in common usage implies majority ownership (50%+).

If the subsidiary is ≥50% owned:
- The Shanghai parent is "subject to the jurisdiction" of a covered nation (PRC)
- Under B.III, the parent is subject to the jurisdiction because it's incorporated in PRC
- But wait, does being "subject to the jurisdiction" make it a FEOC? Let me check.

Under the FEOC definition in B section: an entity is a FEOC if it meets the definition of "foreign entity" AND either is "subject to the jurisdiction" of a covered nation government OR is "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the government of a covered nation.

So the Shanghai parent: foreign entity (organized under PRC laws) + subject to jurisdiction of PRC = FEOC.

Now, is the Indonesian subsidiary a FEOC?
- Under B.III, subsidiaries of a FEOC are not automatically FEOCs solely based on parent being a covered nation jurisdictional entity
- But the subsidiary could be a FEOC if it is (1) subject to jurisdiction of covered nation, or (2) controlled by covered nation government
- The Indonesian subsidiary is organized under Indonesian law, not in a covered nation
- Is it "owned by, controlled by" a covered nation government? The parent is a private Shanghai company. The parent itself is a FEOC by virtue of being subject to PRC jurisdiction.

Wait, but the control test in B.IV is about being "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" a "government of a foreign country that is a covered nation." The parent is a private company, not the government itself. However, the parent IS a FEOC.

Hmm, but the control prong specifically requires control by "a government of a foreign country that is a covered nation." The Shanghai company is private. Is the Shanghai company "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the PRC government?

Actually, let me re-read B.IV more carefully. The entity is "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction" of "another entity (including the government of a foreign country that is a covered nation)." No wait, let me re-read. The FEOC definition requires being "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the jurisdiction or direction of a government of a foreign country that is a covered nation."

So the key is: control by "a government of a foreign country that is a covered nation."

The question says "a private Shanghai company" - it's private, so not government-owned. But it's subject to the jurisdiction of PRC (incorporated in Shanghai).

Under B.III: "a foreign entity is 'subject to the jurisdiction' of a covered nation government if: (i) The foreign entity is incorporated or domiciled in, or has its principal place of business in, a covered nation."

So the Shanghai company is subject to the jurisdiction of PRC → FEOC under the jurisdiction prong.

Now for the Indonesian subsidiary:
- Under B.III: "when an entity is a FEOC due to it being 'subject to the jurisdiction' of a covered nation, subsidiaries of the FEOC are not automatically considered to also be FEOCs solely based on their parent being a covered nation jurisdictional entity."

So the Indonesian subsidiary is NOT automatically a FEOC just because its parent is subject to PRC jurisdiction.

But could the subsidiary be a FEOC via the "owned by, controlled by" prong? The parent is a private Shanghai company. The control prong requires control by "a government of a foreign country that is a covered nation." The private Shanghai company is NOT the government.

However, looking at B.I paragraph (iv): "An entity organized under the laws of the United States that is owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction (as interpreted in Section IV) of an entity that qualifies as a foreign entity in paragraphs (i)-(iii)."

This applies to US entities. The Indonesian subsidiary is not a US entity. But it IS a foreign entity under paragraph (iii) since it's organized under Indonesian law.

So the Indonesian subsidiary is a foreign entity. Is it "subject to the jurisdiction" of a covered nation? No - it's incorporated in Indonesia.

Is it "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the government of a covered nation? The parent is private, not the government. But... is the parent itself "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the PRC government? The question says "private" so presumably not controlled by the government.

Actually wait - the question says "a private Shanghai company's Indonesian subsidiary." The key question is whether the Indonesian subsidiary is "controlled by" a covered nation government. The subsidiary's parent is a private company in Shanghai. The parent itself is a FEOC by virtue of being "subject to the jurisdiction" of PRC. But being subject to jurisdiction ≠ being "owned by, controlled by, or subject to the direction of" the government.

So the Indonesian subsidiary would be: foreign entity (organized under Indonesian law) but NOT subject to jurisdiction of covered nation AND NOT owned/controlled/directed by covered nation government (since the parent is private).

Therefore: **The Indonesian subsidiary is NOT a FEOC.** ✓ (non-FEOC)

Under B.III: "when an entity is a FEOC due to it being 'subject to the jurisdiction' of a covered nation, subsidiaries of the FEOC are not automatically considered to also be FEOCs solely based on their parent being a covered nation jurisdictional entity."

And B.IV: "a FEOC that is controlled by a covered nation government may hold an interest in a subsidiary... and that subsidiary may still not be a FEOC if the covered nation's level of control of the subsidiary falls below 25%."

So even if the Shanghai parent is a FEOC, the Indonesian subsidiary is NOT automatically a FEOC. The subsidiary would only be a FEOC if the covered nation government itself has ≥25% control of the subsidiary.
